Quantum Boltzmann dynamics and bosonized particle-hole interactions in fermion gases
Esteban Cárdenas, Thomas Chen

Abstract
. In this paper, we study a cold gas of N ≫ 1 N\gg 1 weakly interacting fermions. We describe the time evolution of states that are perturbations of the Fermi ball, and analyze the dynamics in particle-hole variables. Our main result states that, for small values of the coupling constant and for appropriate initial data, the effective dynamics of the momentum distribution is determined by a discrete collision operator of quantum Boltzmann form.



1.
Introduction

The quantum Boltzmann equation was first proposed
on phenomenological grounds
by Nordheim
[
41
]
and Uehling and Uhlenbeck
[
48
]

as a quantum-mechanical correction to the classical Boltzmann equation, taking into account the statistics of the particles.
For a spatially homogeneous gas of fermions in three dimensions,
it reads

(1.1)

\displaystyle\partial_{T}F=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{9}}b(p\,p_{*}p^{\prime}p_{*}^{\prime})\Big(F^{\prime}F^{\prime}_{*}(1-F)(1-F_{*})-FF_{*}(1-F^{\prime})(1-F_{*}^{\prime})\Big)\mathrm{d}p_{*}\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\mathrm{d}p_{*}^{\prime}

Here, the unknown
F=F_{T}(p)
is a probability density function in momentum space,
and we use the short-hand notations
F=F(p)
,
F^{\prime}=F(p^{\prime})
,
F_{*}=F(p_{*})

and
F_{*}^{\prime}=F(p_{*}^{\prime})
.
The scattering amplitude is calculated quantum-mechanically in Born approximation
and takes the form (up to geometric constants)

(1.2)

b(p\,p_{*}p^{\prime},p_{*}^{\prime})=\pi\delta(p+p_{*}-p^{\prime}-p^{\prime}_{*})\,\delta\big(p^{2}+p_{*}^{2}-(p^{\prime})^{2}-(p_{*}^{\prime})^{2}\big)\,\big|\hat{v}(p-p^{\prime})-\hat{v}(p-p_{*}^{\prime})\big|^{2}

where
\hat{v}(k)
is the Fourier transform of the microscopic
interaction potential.

Despite important mathematical efforts,
the rigorous derivation of (
1.1
)
from first principles
remains an important open problem in mathematical physics.
This amounts to analyzing the
microscopic dynamics of
an interacting particle system governed by the Schrödinger equation,
and the standing conjecture
states
that the equation
(
1.1
)
emerges
in a suitable scaling limit.

In this regard, one of
the main line of research consists
of studying the
weak coupling limit

of an interacting quantum gas.
Here,
one considers a
particle system with an interaction of strength

\lambda>0
.
Then, the following
macroscopic scaling is implemented

(1.3)

X=\varepsilon x\ ,\qquad T=\varepsilon t\ ,\qquad\lambda=\varepsilon^{1/2}\ .

The time scale

t_{\textnormal{kin}}=\lambda^{-2}T
is known as the
kinetic time-scale
,
and it is conjectured to be the minimal time-scale
that produces
O(1)
corrections to the dynamics.
One then studies the limit

\varepsilon\downarrow 0
of the time evolution
of appropriate distribution functions.
We refer the reader to

[
3
,
5
,
6
,
32
,
38
,
44
]

for further reading.
See also

[
22
,
23
]

for new results proven under additional
conditional regularity assumptions.
To this day, the derivation of the
quantum Boltzmann equation
in the weak-coupling limit
remains an open problem.

In this article, we turn to the study of a different question:

Is there a scaling window
for which
the many-body fermionic dynamics
is–to leading order–governed by an operator
of the form (
1.1
), with complete error analysis?

We refer to this question as the

emergence
of quantum Boltzmann dynamics.

1.1.
Description of main results

Let us informally
describe the main results of this paper,
which aims towards an answer of the proposed question.

We consider the microscopic dynamics
of
N
identical scalar fermions
on the torus of side length
L>0

in dimension
d\geqslant 1
,
which we denote by

\Lambda=(\mathbb{R}/L\mathbb{Z})^{d}\ .

The states are
N
-body wave functions belonging to
the Hilbert space

(1.4)

L^{2}_{a}(\Lambda^{N})=\big\{\Psi\in L^{2}(\Lambda^{N}):\Psi(x_{\sigma(1)},\ldots,x_{\sigma(N)})=(-1)^{\sigma}\Psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\ \forall\sigma\in S_{N}\big\}.

Here,
S_{N}
is the group of permutations of
N
elements; the antisymmetry of the functions
\Psi\in L_{a}^{2}(\Lambda^{N})
characterize
the fermionic statistics of the particles.
We then study the solutions of the Schrödinger equation,
written in microscopic units as

(1.5)

i\partial_{t}\Psi=\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{i}(-\Delta_{x_{i}})+\frac{\lambda}{2}\sum_{i\neq j}V(x_{i}-x_{j})\,\bigg)\Psi\ ,\qquad\Psi(0)=\Psi_{0}\in L^{2}_{a}(\Lambda^{N})\ .

Here,
\lambda>0
is the coupling constant of
the interaction, mediated by a pair potential
V(x-y)
,
and the initial datum is denoted by
\Psi_{0}
.
We denote by

\Lambda^{*}\equiv(\frac{2\pi}{L}\mathbb{Z})^{d}

the dual momentum lattice,
and write for convenience

\int_{\Lambda_{*}}\mathrm{d}p\equiv\frac{1}{|\Lambda|}\sum_{p\in\Lambda^{*}}
.

We consider quasi-free, translation invariant initial data
\Psi_{0}

corresponding to states that are perturbations
of the
Fermi ball
(or,
Fermi sea
)

(1.6)

\Psi_{F}\equiv\bigwedge_{p\in\mathcal{B}}e_{p}\qquad\textnormal{where}\qquad\mathcal{B}\equiv\{p\in(2\pi\mathbb{Z}/L)^{d}:|p|\leqslant p_{F}\}\ ,

in the sense of Condition
2
.
Here, we denote by

e_{p}(x)=|\Lambda|^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{ip\cdot x}

the plane-waves and

p_{F}>0
stands for the
Fermi momentum
.
We
tune
N>0

so that
|\mathcal{B}|=N

and thus, consequently, for fixed
L>0

(1.7)

p_{F}=C_{d}\bigg(\frac{N}{|\Lambda|}\bigg)^{1/d}(1+o(1))\qquad N\rightarrow\infty\ ,

for some constant
C_{d}>0
.
As is well-known, the Fermi ball

\Psi_{F}
corresponds to the non-interacting ground state of the system. It is, therefore,
an approximately stationary solution of the Schrödinger equation for small values of
\lambda>0
.
As we explain below,
we study the dynamics of
\Psi(t)
relative to the Fermi ball
\Psi_{F}
in the particle-hole formalism.

The main object
of interest in this article
corresponds
to the
momentum distribution of the wave-function
\Psi(t)
.
We shall denote if by
F_{t}(p)
, where
p
belongs to the dual lattice

\Lambda^{*}
,
and verifies

(1.8)

\int_{\Lambda_{*}}F_{t}(p)\mathrm{d}p=N\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad 0\leqslant F_{t}(p)\leqslant 1\ .

For a precise definition of
F_{t}(p)
, see (
2.5
) in the next section where we introduce the Second Quantization formalism.
In particular, we are interested in understanding the emergence of
quantum Boltzmann dynamics
for the time evolution of
F_{t}(p)

for small values of the coupling constant
\lambda>0
,
large values of time variable
t\in\mathbb{R}
, and at fixed length
L>0
.

In this regard, our main result is contained in Theorem
3

and establishes the emergence
of the quantum Boltzmann operator (
1.1
)
for the dynamics of
F_{t}(p)
,
in a particular scaling window.
We consider the following scaling
for
d=3

(1.9)

\lambda=N^{-\frac{3}{2}-\delta}\qquad t=N^{\frac{1}{6}+\frac{\delta}{2}}\,T\ ,\qquad\textnormal{and }\ L>0\ \textnormal{ fixed}

where
\delta\in(0,1)
is small enough, and

F_{0}
is a small enough
perturbation of the Fermi ball.
The coupling in the
scaling (
1.9
) is chosen
so that higher-order
many-body effects can be controlled.
At the same time, the time-scale in (
1.9
)
is chosen to be large enough
so that completed collisions can be detected.

In the scaling (
1.9
),
we prove the validity of the following asymptotic expansion

(1.10)

F_{t}=F_{0}+(\lambda t)^{2}\,\mathscr{C}[F_{0}]+\cdots

with respect to an appropriate metric,
with rigorous control of the error terms.
The
operator in (
1.10
)
is a
discrete version of the right hand side of
(
1.1
), given by

(1.11)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}[F]\equiv\int_{(\Lambda_{*})^{3}}b(p\,p_{*}p^{\prime}p_{*}^{\prime})\Big(F^{\prime}F^{\prime}_{*}(1-F)(1-F_{*})-FF_{*}(1-F^{\prime})(1-F_{*}^{\prime})\Big)\mathrm{d}p_{*}\mathrm{d}p^{\prime}\mathrm{d}p_{*}^{\prime}

where now
the scattering
ampliute
contains a
discrete

energy-conservation delta function

(1.12)

\displaystyle b(pp_{*}

\displaystyle p^{\prime}p^{\prime}_{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi}{(\pi/2)}\,\delta(p+p_{*}-p^{\prime}-p^{\prime}_{*})\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}\big(p^{2}+p_{*}^{2}-(p^{\prime})^{2}-(p_{*}^{\prime})^{2}\big)|\widehat{V}(p-p^{\prime})-\widehat{V}(p-p_{*}^{\prime})|^{2}

where we denote

\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}(x)\equiv\delta_{x,0}

and

\delta(p-q)=|\Lambda|^{-1}\delta_{p,q}
.
In particular,
we note that
the leading order term grows
quadratically
with time,
and includes an additional factor
\pi/2

relative to (
1.2
).
See Remark
1.2
below for a discussion.
Here we have a factor
\pi
rather than the conventional
4\pi
thanks
to the
\frac{\lambda}{2}
in (
1.5
).

1.2.
Discussion

Remark 1.1
.

Throughout this paper,
the side length of the box
L
is kept fixed.
Thus, we obtain a
discrete operator
\mathscr{C}

rather than its continuous counterpart (
1.1
).
Notice that this is a high-density regime, and the Fermi momentum
p_{F}>0
becomes large.
In contrast, the weak-coupling limit
considers systems with constant density.

Remark 1.2
.

Let us comment on the quadratic time dependence
of the
leading order term of (
1.10
).
Recall that
in quantum mechanical systems,
the time-energy Heisenberg uncertainty principle states
that energy conservation cannot
hold exactly at finite microscopic times.
In this regard, the
relevant time-energy delta function
that appears naturally
in collision operators is

(1.13)

\displaystyle\delta_{t}(E)\equiv t\delta_{1}(tE)\qquad\textnormal{ where }\qquad\delta_{1}(E)\equiv\frac{2}{\pi}\frac{\sin^{2}\big(\frac{E}{2}\big)}{E^{2}}

for
t,E\in\mathbb{R}
.

It is well-known
that after the limit

L\rightarrow\infty
the function
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\delta_{t}(E)

becomes a Dirac delta
\delta(E)
.
Indeed, for illustration purposes, consider a smooth
test function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

and note that

(1.14)

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{L\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{L}\sum_{n\in\frac{1}{L}\mathbb{Z}}\delta_{t}(n)\varphi(n)=\varphi(0)\ .

In this paper, we consider fixed
L>0

and, consequently,
the same quantity behaves like
O(t)
for large times.
Taking
L=1
for illustration yields

(1.15)

\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\delta_{t}(n)\varphi(n)=\frac{t}{\pi/2}\ \varphi(0)+O(1/t)\qquad\textnormal{as}\qquad t\rightarrow\infty

which we interpret as a lattice effect.
See for reference Lemma
10.1
.
As a consequence of (
1.15
),
the leading order term
in (
1.10
) is
O(\lambda^{2}t^{2})
;
much larger than the expected
O(\lambda^{2}t)
in the macroscopic limit (
1.14
).

Remark 1.3
.

We consider initial data
F_{0}

for which
\mathscr{C}[F_{0}]
is of order
N^{1/3}

in an appropriate norm.
Thus, the first order correction
to the momentum distribution
in (
1.10
)
in the scaling (
1.9
)
is of order

(1.16)

O(\lambda^{2}t^{2}N^{1/3})=O(N^{-7/3-\delta})

While this correction is certainly small,
the leading order term in the expansion (
1.10
)
is determined by the quantum Boltzmann operator

\mathscr{C}[F_{0}]
,
with full error control over the subleading terms.

1.3.
Strategy

The heart of our approach is
the study of the time evolution
of the momentum distribution
F_{t}(p)

relative to the stationary Fermi ball.
It is then very convenient
to introduce the momentum distributions
of
\Psi_{F}\in L_{a}^{2}(\Lambda^{N})
as well as its complement:

(1.17)

\chi\equiv\mathds{1}(|p|\leqslant p_{F})\qquad\textnormal{ and }\qquad\chi^{\perp}\equiv 1-\chi\ .

We
change variables
and work in
particle-hole space.
Namely, we study the
the momentum distribution
f_{t}(p)
of excited particles and empty holes (see Definition
1
)
which satisfies

(1.18)

F_{t}(p)=\begin{cases}f_{t}(p)\quad&|p|>p_{F}\\
1-f_{t}(p)\quad&|p|\leqslant p_{F}\end{cases}\ .

Our attention is on states that arise as external perturbations of the system.
See Condition
2
for more details.

The proof of the expansion (
1.10
) can then be broken down into three steps.

(1)

In Theorem
1

we study the dynamics of
f_{t}(p)

in terms of the
unconstrained parameters
of the theory
and find a general estimate in (
2.38
) in dimension
d\geqslant 1
.

(2)

In Theorem
2
we especialize this estimate to the scaling (
1.9
) in
d=3

and show that the remainder terms are smaller in an appropriate sense, and for well-chosen initial data.
Here, extracting the dependence of the collision operators with respect to
t\gg 1
is essential.

(3)

In Theorem
3
we go back to the momentum distribution
F_{t}(p)

and conclude the validity of the expansion (
1.10
).

Let us now describe these steps in more detail.

Step 1.

Here, our main goal is to analyze the effective dynamics of
f_{t}(p)
,
which is driven by a particle-hole Hamiltonian
\mathfrak{h}
described
in Section
3
.

In Theorem
1
,
we consider
the following asymptotic expansion in terms of the coupling
\lambda>0

and microscopic time
t\in\mathbb{R}

(1.19)

f_{t}=f_{0}+\lambda^{2}t\,B_{t}[f_{0}]+\lambda^{2}t\,Q_{t}[f_{0}]+\cdots\

and give an explicit estimate on the remainder,
in terms of the unconstrained parameters of the theory.
In particular, our estimates focus on the description of the dynamics of fermions whose momenta are
away
from the Fermi surface.
This is encoded in the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\ell_{m}^{1*}}
that we use to measure distances.
Note that this consistent with the fact that
particles and holes near the Fermi surface couple together to form
bosonized
particle-hole pairs,
and experience different dynamical behaviour.
This bosonization phenomenon was first explained by
Sawada
[
45
]
and Sawada, Brueckner, Fukuda and Brout
[
46
]

in the 1950s,
and has recently been proven rigorously in a series of papers
[
8
,
9
,
10
,
13
,
35
,
25
,
26
,
27
]
.

The operator
B_{t}
is quadratic and is given in Definition
2
.
It describes the interaction between fermions that are mediated
by
virtual
bosonized particle-hole pairs near the Fermi surface.
In particular, our assumptions on the initial data and on the dynamics
guarantee that the Fermi surface is
depleted

throughout the time scale under consideration
i.e. interactions with real particle-hole pairs are negligible (see Proposition
5.2
).
On the other hand, the operator
Q_{t}
is given in Definition
3
, and corresponds to an energy-mollified
collisional operator of quantum Boltzmann form,
describing
collisions of the form: particle-particle, particle-hole, and hole-hole.

Both operators satisfy
B_{t}\big|_{t=0}=Q_{t}\big|_{t=0}=0
.
Therefore, they do
not
dominate over the remainders
for small times.
In the next step, we consider longer time scales
and extract the leading order time dependence.

Step 2
.
In Theorem
2

we consider the dynamics for longer time scales,
and for small values of the coupling.
More precisely, we specialize to the
scaling (
1.9
)
in three dimensions.
The scaling is chosen so that
\lambda
is small enough to control error terms arising from Theorem
1
,
but
t
is large enough to extract the leading order terms of
B_{t}
and
Q_{t}
.
In particular, it is long enough to observe completed collisions.

We identify the leading order terms of the operators

B_{t}
and
Q_{t}
as
t\rightarrow\infty
, with rigorous error control.
These are given by the formal limits

(1.20)

\mathscr{B}:=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{\lambda\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}B_{t}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\mathscr{Q}:=\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{\lambda\downarrow 0}\frac{1}{t}Q_{t}\ .

Observe that we take the factor
\frac{1}{t}

in order to extract the leading order term, in agreement with
(
1.15
). In particular, after we take the limits
t\rightarrow\infty
,
it is possible to establish
lower bounds

for the operators
\mathscr{B}

and
\mathscr{Q}
.
This then allows for comparison with the remainder terms obtained in Theorem
1
.
For small enough perturbations,
we construct initial data
such that in an
\ell^{\infty}
-norm:

(1.21)

\mathscr{B}[f_{0}]\,\sim\,N^{1/3}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\mathscr{Q}[f_{0}]\,\lesssim\,N^{1/6}\ .

Step 3.
Finally, in Theorem
3
we use the relation (
1.18
)
to go back to the original momentum distribution function
F_{t}(p)
.
In particular, one must here compare
\mathscr{B}[f]
and
\mathscr{C}[F]
.
We find that for appropriate perturbations,
there holds

(1.22)

\mathscr{C}[F]\ =\ (1-\chi)\mathscr{B}[f]\ -\ \chi\mathscr{B}[f]

modulo a small error term, see e.g Proposition
11.1
.
A combination of (
1.22
) and Theorem
2
allows us to finish the proof of (
1.10
).

From a conceptual level, the last observation is crucial.
Namely, it states that the emergence of the operator that drives the quantum Boltzmann equation (
1.1
)
for states
F
near the Fermi ball,
is associated to a
quadratic
operator
\mathscr{B}
in particle-hole space,
describing interactions with a boson-field, i.e. the bosonized particle-hole pairs.

1.4.
Organization of this article

In Section
2
we state the main result of this article,
and in Section
3
we introduce the preliminaries that are needed to set up the proof.
In Sections
4
and
5

we introduce and develop the machinery
that we use in our analysis.
In Section
6
and
7
we show how the operators
Q
and
B
,
respectively, emerge from the many-body dynamics, giving rise to the
leading order terms
.
In Section
8
we estimate
subleading order terms
and in Section
9
we prove our main result, Theorem
1
.
Finally, in Section
10
we analyze the fixed volume case.

1.5.
Notation

The following notation is going to be used throughout this article.

-

\Lambda^{*}\equiv(2\pi\mathbb{Z}/L)^{d}
denotes the dual lattice of
\Lambda
.

-

We write
\int_{\Lambda^{*}}F(p)\mathrm{d}p\equiv|\Lambda|^{-1}\sum_{p\in\Lambda^{*}}F(p)

for any function
F:\Lambda^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

-

\delta_{p,q}
denotes the standard Kronecker delta.

-

We denote

\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}(x)=\delta_{x,0}

and

\delta(p-q)=|\Lambda|\delta_{p,q}
.

-

\ell^{p}(\Lambda^{*})
denotes the space of functions
with finite norm
\|f\|_{\ell^{p}}\equiv(\int_{\Lambda^{*}}|f(p)|^{p}\mathrm{d}p)^{1/p}
.

-

B(X)
denotes the space of bounded linear operators acting on
X
.

-

We denote
\widetilde{f}\equiv 1-f
for any function
f:\Lambda^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.

-

\widehat{G}(k)\equiv(2\pi)^{-d/2}\int_{\Lambda}e^{-ik\cdot x}G(x)\mathrm{d}x

denotes the Fourier transform of
G:\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{C}.

-

We say that a positive real number
C>0
is a
constant
, if it is independent of the physical parameters

N
,
|\Lambda|
,
\lambda
,
n
and
t
.

-

Given two real-valued quantities
A

and

B
,
we say that
A\lesssim B
if there exists a constant
C>0

such that

(1.23)

\displaystyle A\leqslant C\,B\ .

Additionally, we say that
A\simeq B
if both
A\lesssim B
and
B\lesssim A
hold true.

-

We shall frequently omit subscripts from Hilbert spaces
norms throughout proofs.

-

We denote by
\left\langle t\right\rangle=(1+t^{2})^{1/2}
the standard bracket.

2.
Main results

The main result of this article is a rigorous interpretation of the expansion
(
1.10
) that arises from the many-body fermionic dynamics.
First, we present the model that we study and introduce particle-hole variables.
Secondly, we present our first
main result in Theorem
1
.
It contains an estimate
in a weighted
\ell^{\infty}
norm for the error of the momentum distribution of particles and holes.
Thirdly, in Theorem
2
we discuss the consequences of this estimate in a precise scaling.
Here, we extract the leading order terms
and prove appropriate lower bounds.
Finally, in Theorem
3
we go back to the momentum distribution
F_{t}(p)
.

2.1.
The model

We consider the torus

\Lambda\equiv(\mathbb{R}/L\mathbb{Z})^{d}
where
L>0
is its linear length.
We study the dynamics on the
Fock space

(2.1)

\textstyle\mathscr{F}\equiv\mathbb{C}\oplus\bigoplus_{n\geqslant 1}\mathscr{F}_{n}\qquad{\textnormal{where}}\qquad\mathscr{F}_{n}\equiv\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}L^{2}(\Lambda),\ \forall n\geqslant 1\ .

As usual,
\mathscr{F}
is equipped with creation and annihilation operators
a_{p}
and
a_{q}^{*}
.
In momentum space
that satisfy the Canonical Anticommutation Relations (CAR)

(2.2)

\{a_{p},a_{q}^{*}\}=\delta(p-q)\equiv|\Lambda|\delta_{p,q}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\{a_{p}^{*},a_{q}^{*}\}=\{a_{p},a_{q}\}=0

for all
p,q\in\Lambda^{*}\equiv(2\pi\mathbb{Z}/L)^{d}
.
Here,
\delta_{p,q}
stands for the Kronecker delta and
and
\{\cdot,\cdot\}
denotes the anticommutator.
The Fock vacuum vector will be denoted by
\Omega\in\mathscr{F}
, and
for notational convenience we denote sums by

\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\mathrm{d}p\equiv|\Lambda|^{-1}\sum_{p\in\Lambda^{*}}
.

The Hamiltonian on Fock space
that we study corresponds to the
operator

(2.3)

\mathcal{H}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Lambda^{*}}|p|^{2}a_{p}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p+\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{(\Lambda^{*})^{3}}\hat{V}(k)\ a^{*}_{p+k}a^{*}_{q-k}a_{q}a_{p}\ \mathrm{d}p\,\mathrm{d}q\,\mathrm{d}k\ ,

where
\hat{V}(k)\equiv(2\pi)^{-3/2}\int_{\Lambda}V(x)\mathrm{d}x
is the Fourier transform of a two-body potential

V(x)
.
We are interested in the
dynamics generated by
\mathcal{H}
on initial states
that are appropriate
perturbations of the Fermi ball . These are described
in Condition
2

and can be understood as
external perturbations
of states
with momentum distribution
\chi(p)
.

Let us describe the time evolution generated by the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
, defined in (
2.3
).
Denote by
B(\mathscr{F})
the
C^{*}
-algebra of bounded operators on Fock space,
and consider an initial state
\rho
.
Then, the dynamics of the system is given by

(2.4)

\rho_{t}(\mathcal{O})=\rho(e^{it\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{O}e^{-it\mathcal{H}})\ ,\qquad\mathcal{O}\in B(\mathscr{F})\ .

Here and in the sequel, the time variable
t\in\mathbb{R}
should be understood as being measured in microscopic units.
In particular, the momentum distribution per unit volume of the system is defined as

(2.5)

F_{t}(p)\equiv|\Lambda|^{-1}\rho_{t}(a_{p}^{*}a_{p})\ ,

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in\Lambda^{*}
.

The initial states that we study are external perturbation of the
Fermi ball.
That is,
\rho
corresponds to a translation invariant state,
which is a
suitable perturbation
of the pure state

(2.6)

\Psi_{F}=\prod_{p\in\mathcal{B}}a^{*}_{p}\Omega\qquad\textnormal{where}\qquad\mathcal{B}=\{p\in\Lambda^{*}:|p|\leqslant p_{F}\}\ .

The state
\Psi_{F}

corresponds to the Slater determinant
of plane waves

e_{p}(x)=|\Lambda|^{-1/2}e^{ip\cdot x}

with momenta in
\mathcal{B}
,
minimizing the kinetic energy of the system in compliance with
Pauli’s exclusion principle.

We refer interchangeably to
\Psi_{F}
and
\mathcal{B}
as the
Fermi ball
(or,
Fermi sea
),
defined in terms of the Fermi momentum
p_{F}
.
For simplicity, we assume here that
p_{F}
is given, and define the number of particles to be
N\equiv|\mathcal{B}|
.
The relationship between
p_{F}
and
N
is then given by the formula

(2.7)

p_{F}\,=\,C\big(N/|\Lambda|\big)^{1/d}\ ,

where
C=C_{d}+o(1)
is a constant depending only on the dimension, and lower order terms on
p_{F}
.
In particular, it is important to note that the high-density regime that we study
corresponds to large values of the Fermi momentum
p_{F}\gg 1
.

2.2.
Assumptions and definitions

Let us state the precise mathematical conditions under which our main theorems are formulated.
We first discuss the conditions on the interaction potentials, and then the conditions on the initial data.

Potentials.

Throughout this work,
we shall consider real-valued functions

V:\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

that satisfy Condition
1
below.
In particular, under these conditions, the many-body Hamiltonian

\mathcal{H}
defined in (
2.3
) is self-adjoint, and
its dynamics is well-defined.

Condition 1
.

V:\Lambda\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

is a real-valued function
whose
Fourier transform
\hat{V}(k)

satisfies the following conditions.

(1)

It has compact support in a ball of radius
r>0
.

(2)

\hat{V}(-k)=\hat{V}(k)
for all
k\in\Lambda^{*}
. Thus,
\hat{V}
is real-valued .

(3)

\hat{V}(0)=0
.

(4)

V
is chosen relative to the box
\Lambda

so that
\sup_{|\Lambda|>0}\|\hat{V}\|_{\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*})}<\infty
.

Remark 2.1
.

The radius
r>0
will be fundamental in our analysis.
From a physical point of view, it determines a momentum scale
that allows for particle interactions.
In particular, it will determine an
\mathcal{O}(1)
neighborhood around the Fermi surface which separates excited particles and holes,
into either bosonizable or non-bosonizable.

States
.
The initial states
that we consider are regarded as external perturbations of the Fermi ball

\Psi_{F}
.
It will be convenient to use
the following notations
for the momentum distribution
of
\Psi_{F}
, as well as its complement

(2.8)

\chi(p)=\mathds{1}(|p|\leqslant p_{F})\qquad\textnormal{ and }\qquad\chi^{\perp}=1-\chi\ ,

where
\mathds{1}
stands for a characteristic function. In particular, it is now a standard calculation using the
CAR to show that

(2.9)

\left\langle\Psi_{F},a_{p}^{*}a_{q}\Psi_{F}\right\rangle=\delta(p-q)\chi(p)

for all
p,q\in\Lambda^{*}
.

We analyze the dynamics of fermions
relative to the Fermi ball.
In this regard, we think of
excited fermions with momentum
|p|>p_{F}

as
particles
,
and the anti-particles
they leave behind inside of the Fermi ball as

holes
, with momentum
|h|\leqslant p_{F}

This change of variables is implemented by a

particle-hole transformation
.
It corresponds to the unitary transformation on Fock space

(2.10)

\mathcal{R}:\mathscr{F}\longrightarrow\mathscr{F}

that
can be explicitly defined
through its action on creation- and annihilation operators as follows

(2.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{*}a_{p}^{*}\mathcal{R}\ =\ \begin{cases}\ a_{p}^{*}\qquad|p|>p_{F}\\
\ a_{p}\qquad|p|\leqslant p_{F}\end{cases}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\mathcal{R}\Omega\equiv\Psi_{F}\ .

In particular, we are interested in describing
the time evolution of the momentum distribution of states relative to the Fermi ball.
In particle-hole space, the dynamics of these states is described by the

particle-hole Hamiltonian

(2.12)

\mathfrak{h}\equiv\mathcal{R}^{*}\mathcal{H}\mathcal{R}\ .

A more explicit representation of the Hamiltonian
\mathfrak{h}
will be given in the next section.

Thus, we study the evolution-in-time of the
corresponding momentum distribution, defined as follows.
Recall that a state is a positive linear functional on
B(\mathscr{F})
, with

\nu(\mathds{1})=1
.

Definition 1
.

Given an initial state
\nu
, we
define
the momentum distribution per unit volume of particles and holes
as

(2.13)

\displaystyle f_{t}(p)\equiv|\Lambda|^{-1}\nu(e^{\mathrm{i}t\mathfrak{h}}a_{p}^{*}a_{p}e^{-\mathrm{i}t\mathfrak{h}})\ ,

for
(t,p)\in\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda^{*}
.

Remark 2.2
.

Let
F_{t}(p)
be the momentum distribution (
2.5
)
of a state
\rho
,
evolving accrording to the Hamiltonian
\mathcal{H}
, in the original many-body problem.
Then, a straightforward calculation using the CAR shows that

(2.14)

F_{t}(p)=\begin{cases}f_{t}(p)\quad&|p|>p_{F}\\
1-f_{t}(p)\quad&|p|\leqslant p_{F}\ ,\end{cases}

where
f_{t}(p)
is given as in Definition
1
,
with respect to the unitarily transformed initial state

(2.15)

\nu(\mathcal{O})\equiv\rho(\mathcal{R}\mathcal{O}\mathcal{R}^{*})\ ,\qquad\mathcal{O}\in B(\mathscr{F})\ .

Note that, if
\rho
and
\nu
are determined by pure states

\Psi
and
\psi
, respectively, then
(
2.15
)
is equivalent to

\Psi=\mathcal{R}\psi
.
In particular,
\Psi=\Psi_{F}
if and only if
\psi=\Omega
.

We find it convenient to introduce conditions on the initial data with respect to the particle-hole variables.
In order to state them,
recall that the interaction potential
\hat{V}

has support of size
r>0
.
We introduce the following neighborhood around the surface of the Fermi ball

(2.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\equiv\{p\in\Lambda^{*}:p_{F}-3r\leqslant|p|\leqslant p_{F}+3r\}

which (under a slight abuse of notation) we shall refer to as the
Fermi surface
.
The pre-factor
3
is included for technical reasons.

The conditions for the initial data in the particle-hole representation are given as follows.

Condition 2
.

The initial state
\nu
verifies the following conditions.

(C1)

\nu
is a mixed state: there exists sequences
0\leqslant\nu_{j}\leqslant 1
and
\Psi_{j}\in\mathscr{F}

such that
\nu(\mathcal{O})=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\nu_{j}\left\langle\Psi_{j},\mathcal{O}\Psi_{j}\right\rangle_{\mathscr{F}}

with
\sum\nu_{j}=1
and
\|\Psi_{j}\|_{\mathscr{F}}=1
.

(C2)

\nu
is number-conserving and quasi-free:
for all
k,k^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
,
p_{1},\ldots,p_{k}\in\Lambda^{*}
and
q_{1},\ldots,q_{k^{\prime}}\in\Lambda^{*}
there holds

(2.17)

\nu\Big(a_{p_{1}}^{*}\cdots a_{p_{k}}^{*}a_{q_{k^{\prime}}}\cdots a_{q_{1}}\Big)\,=\,\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}\det\big[\nu(a_{p_{i}}^{*}a_{q_{j}})\big]_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant k}\ .

(C3)

\nu
is translation invariant:
for all
p,q\in\Lambda^{*}
there holds

\nu(a_{p}^{*}a_{q})=\delta(p-q)\nu(a_{p}^{*}a_{p})\ .

(C4)

\nu
has zero charge:

\int_{\mathcal{B}}\nu(a_{p}^{*}a_{p})\mathrm{d}p=\int_{\mathcal{B}^{c}}\nu(a_{p}^{*}a_{p})\mathrm{d}p\ .

(C5)

There exists a constant
C\geqslant 0
such that

\int_{\mathcal{S}}\nu(a_{p}^{*}a_{p})\mathrm{d}p\leqslant C(\lambda|\Lambda|p_{F}^{d-1})^{2}
.

Remark 2.3
.

Analogously as in
[
32
]
,
we could have considered states that are only restricted quasi-free up to a certain degree,
and our main results would remain unchanged.
Since all the examples we consider quasi-free, we leave it as is.

We note that translation invariance is a natural assumption that greatly simplifies the analysis, while at the same time being physically relevant.
The same comments apply to the requirement of states having zero charge.

Finally, let us comment on Condition (C5). We refer to it as the depletion of the Fermi surface,
and it makes precise the idea that the initial data
we consider
arises as an external perturbation to the system.
Namely, the support of the distribution

f_{0}(p)

contains only highly excited particles and holes,
and only small thermal fluctuations
around the surface are allowed.

Example.

We may construct a pure state
\nu
that verifies Condition
2

as a Slater determinant.
Namely, given
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
let
h_{1},\ldots,h_{n}\in\mathcal{B}\backslash\mathcal{S}

and

p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}\in\mathcal{B}^{c}\backslash\mathcal{S}
.
Then, we set

(2.18)

\nu(\mathcal{O})\equiv\left\langle\Psi_{0},\mathcal{O}\Psi_{0}\right\rangle_{\mathscr{F}}\qquad\textnormal{where}\qquad\Psi_{0}\equiv a^{*}_{h_{1}}\cdots a^{*}_{h_{n}}a^{*}_{p_{1}}\cdots a^{*}_{p_{n}}\Omega\ .

Since Slater determinants are always number-conserving and quasi-free, this verifies (C2).
One may verify that translation invariance in (C3)
is satisfied by direct computation of the two-point function

(2.19)

\nu(a_{p}^{*}a_{q})=\delta(p-q)\Big(\delta(p-h_{1})+\ldots+\delta(p-h_{n})+\delta(p-p_{1})+\ldots+\delta(p-p_{n})\Big)\ .

The state
\nu
has zero charge in (C4)
because
we have chosen an equal number of
h_{i}^{\prime}s
and
p_{i}^{\prime}s
in

\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{B}^{c}
, respectively.
Finally, (C5) follows from
\nu(a_{p}^{*}a_{p})=0

for all
p\in\mathcal{S}.

2.3.
Statement of the main theorem

Our main result identifies the time evolution of the momentum distribution
f_{t}(p)

in terms of two non-linear operators that act on functions on
\Lambda^{*}
.
In order to define these, we introduce
the following three notations.

Notations

(i)

We denote
by
E_{p}
the
dispersion relation
of particles and holes.
It is defined for
p\in\Lambda^{*}

(2.20)

E_{p}\,\equiv\,-\chi(p)\Big(\ \frac{p^{2}}{2}\,+\,\frac{\lambda}{2}\,(\hat{V}*\chi^{\perp})(p)\ \Big)+\chi^{\perp}(p)\Big(\ \frac{p^{2}}{2}\,-\,\frac{\lambda}{2}\,(\hat{V}*\chi)(p)\ \Big)\ .

(ii)

For
(t,E)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

we denote by
\delta_{t}(E)

the following

mollified Delta function

(2.21)

\displaystyle\delta_{t}(E)=t\delta_{1}(tE)\qquad\textnormal{ where }\qquad\delta_{1}(E)=\frac{2}{\pi}\frac{\sin^{2}\big(\frac{E}{2}\big)}{E^{2}}\ .

In this context, the presence of an approximate delta function is a consequence of the Heisenberg uncertainty principle for the
conjugate variables
(t,E)
of time and energy.

(iii)

Third, we introduce the following convenient notation for products
of
\chi
. Namely, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}

we will write

(2.22)

\chi(p_{1},\ldots,p_{k})\equiv\chi(p_{1})\cdots\chi(p_{k})

for all
p_{1},\cdots,p_{k}\in\Lambda^{*}

and similarly for
\chi^{\perp}.

The following operator describes describes Boltzmann-type interactions
between particles/particles, particles/holes and holes/holes.
Here and in the sequel we denote

\widetilde{f}\equiv 1-f

for any function
f:\Lambda^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
.

Definition 2
.

For all
t\in\mathbb{R}

we define in terms of particle and hole interactions

(2.23)

\displaystyle B_{t}

\displaystyle\equiv B_{t}^{(H)}+B_{t}^{(P)}:\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*})\rightarrow\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*})

where

B_{t}^{(H)}:\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*})\rightarrow\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*})

and

B_{t}^{(P)}:\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*})\rightarrow\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*})

are defined as follows

\displaystyle B_{t}^{(H)}[f](h)

\displaystyle\equiv 2\pi\int_{\Lambda^{*}}|\hat{V}(k)|^{2}\Big(\alpha^{H}_{t}(h-k,k)f(h-k)\widetilde{f}(h)-\alpha^{H}_{t}(h,k)f(h)\widetilde{f}(h+k)\Big)\mathrm{d}h\ ,

\displaystyle B_{t}^{(P)}[f](p)

\displaystyle\equiv 2\pi\int_{\Lambda^{*}}|\hat{V}(k)|^{2}\Big(\alpha^{P}_{t}(p+k,k)f(p+k)\widetilde{f}(p)-\alpha^{P}_{t}(p,k)f(p)\widetilde{f}(p-k)\Big)\mathrm{d}p\ ,

for
f\in\ell^{1}
and
p,h\in\Lambda^{*}
.
Here, the coefficients
\alpha^{H}_{t}
and
\alpha^{P}_{t}
are defined as

(2.24)

\displaystyle\alpha^{H}_{t}(h,k)

\displaystyle\equiv\chi(h)\chi(h+k)\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi(r)\chi^{\perp}(r+k)\delta_{t}\big[E_{h}-E_{h+k}-E_{r}-E_{r+k}\big]\mathrm{d}r\ ,

(2.25)

\displaystyle\alpha^{P}_{t}(p,k)

\displaystyle\equiv\chi^{\perp}(p)\chi^{\perp}(p-k)\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi(r)\chi^{\perp}(r+k)\delta_{t}\big[E_{p}-E_{p-k}-E_{r}-E_{r+k}\big]\mathrm{d}r\ ,

for all
p,h,k\in\Lambda^{*}.

Definition 3
.

For
f\in\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*})

and

t\in\mathbb{R}

we
define

(2.26)

\displaystyle Q_{t}[f](p)

\displaystyle\equiv\pi\int_{\Lambda^{*4}}\mathrm{d}\vec{p}\,\sigma(\vec{p})\,\Big[\delta(p-p_{1})+\delta(p-p_{2})-\delta(p-p_{3})-\delta(p-p_{4})\Big]

\displaystyle\times\delta_{t}[E_{p_{1}}+E_{p_{2}}-E_{p_{3}}-E_{p_{4}}]\Big(f(p_{3})f(p_{4})\widetilde{f}(p_{1})\widetilde{f}(p_{2})-f(p_{1})f(p_{2})\widetilde{f}(p_{3})\widetilde{f}(p_{4})\Big)\ .

The coefficient function
\sigma:(\Lambda^{*})^{4}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is decomposed as

(2.27)

\sigma=\sigma_{HH}+\sigma_{PP}+\sigma_{HP}+\sigma_{PH}

where the coefficient functions are defined for
\vec{p}=(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})\in(\Lambda^{*})^{4}

as follows

(2.28)

\displaystyle\sigma_{HH}(\vec{p})

\displaystyle=\chi(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})\delta(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{3}-p_{4})|\hat{V}(p_{1}-p_{4})-\hat{V}(p_{1}-p_{3})|^{2}

(2.29)

\displaystyle\sigma_{PP}(\vec{p})

\displaystyle=\chi^{\perp}(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3},p_{4})\delta(p_{1}+p_{2}-p_{3}-p_{4})|\hat{V}(p_{1}-p_{4})-\hat{V}(p_{1}-p_{3})|^{2}

(2.30)

\displaystyle\sigma_{HP}(\vec{p})

\displaystyle=2\chi(p_{1},p_{3})\chi^{\perp}(p_{2},p_{4})\delta(p_{1}-p_{2}-p_{3}+p_{4})|\hat{V}(p_{1}-p_{3})|^{2}

(2.31)

\displaystyle\sigma_{PH}(\vec{p})

\displaystyle=2\chi^{\perp}(p_{1},p_{3})\chi(p_{2},p_{4})\delta(p_{1}-p_{2}-p_{3}+p_{4})|\hat{V}(p_{1}-p_{3})|^{2}\ .

Definition of the norm
.
We will analyze error terms with respect to a weighted norm which we now introduce.
Indeed, for
m>0
we introduce the following weight

(2.32)

w_{m}(p)\equiv\begin{cases}\left\langle p\right\rangle^{m}\ ,&p\in\mathcal{S}\\
1\ ,&p\in\Lambda^{*}\backslash\mathcal{S}\end{cases}\ .

where
\left\langle p\right\rangle\equiv(1+p^{2})^{1/2}
denotes the standard Japanese bracket.
We define the Banach space
\ell_{m}^{1}\equiv\ell^{1}_{m}(\Lambda^{*})

of functions
\varphi:\Lambda^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

for which the norm

(2.33)

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{\ell^{1}_{m}}\equiv\int_{\Lambda^{*}}|\varphi(p)|w_{m}(p)\mathrm{d}p

is finite.
We will measure distances in the norm associated to the dual space of
\ell^{1}_{m}(\Lambda^{*})
.
Namely, we regard
\ell_{m}^{1*}\equiv[\ell^{1}_{m}(\Lambda^{*})]^{*}
as the Banach
space of functions
f:\Lambda^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}

endowed with the norm

(2.34)

\|f\|_{\ell^{1*}_{m}}\equiv\sup_{p\in\Lambda^{*}}w_{m}(p)^{-1}|f(p)|=\sup_{\varphi\in\ell^{1}_{m}}\frac{|\left\langle\varphi,f\right\rangle|}{\|\varphi\|_{\ell_{m}^{1}}}

where we denote by
\left\langle\varphi,f\right\rangle\equiv\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\overline{\varphi(p)}f(p)\mathrm{d}p

the coupling between
\ell_{m}^{1}

and
\ell^{1*}_{m}
.

Remark 2.4
.

As vector spaces,

\ell_{m}^{1}(\Lambda^{*})=\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*})

and

\ell_{m}^{1*}(\Lambda^{*})=\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda^{*})
for all
m>0
.
However, we choose to equip these spaces with the norms

\|\cdot\|_{\ell_{m}^{1}}

and

\|\cdot\|_{\ell_{m}^{1*}}

since the weight
w_{m}(p)
appropriately records the decay near the Fermi surface
\mathcal{S}
–this point will be crucial in our analysis.
For completeness, we record here the following inequality

(2.35)

\|f\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda^{*}\backslash\mathcal{S})}\leqslant\|f\|_{\ell_{m}^{1*}}\leqslant\|f\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda^{*})}\ ,\qquad\forall f\in\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda^{*})

which we shall make use of, when studying the fixed volume case in the next subsection.

Remark 2.5
.

If
f\in\ell_{m}^{1*}
is real-valued, one may restrict the supremum over
\varphi\in\ell_{m}^{1}

on the right hand side of (
2.34
) to be real-valued as well.

Finally, let us now introduce
two important new parameters.
Namely,
these are the numbers

n
and
R
defined as

(2.36)

n\equiv|\Lambda|\int_{\Lambda^{*}}f_{0}(p)\mathrm{d}p\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad R\equiv|\Lambda|p_{F}^{d-1}\simeq|\mathcal{S}|\ .

Here,
n
corresponds to the
the initial number of particles and holes in the system,
and it measures the size of the perturbation of the Fermi ball.
On the other hand
R
corresponds
to the maximal number of bosonized particle-hole pairs
that can populate the Fermi surface
\mathcal{S}
, defined in (
2.16
).
Our main result is now stated as follows.

Theorem 1
.

Let
f_{t}(p)
be the momentum distribution of particles and holes,
as given in Definition
1
.
We assume that
Condition
1

and

2
are satisfied,
as well as the bounds
1\leqslant n\leqslant CR^{1/2}
.
Then, for all
m>0
there exists
C=C(m,d)>0
such that
for all
t\geqslant 0

there holds

(2.37)

\displaystyle f_{t}\,=\,f_{0}\,+\,\lambda^{2}t\,B_{t}[f_{0}]\,+\,\lambda^{2}t\,Q_{t}[f_{0}]\,+\,\lambda^{2}t\,\textnormal{Rem}_{1}(t)\ ,

where
\textnormal{Rem}_{1}(t)
is a reminder term that satisfies

(2.38)

\|\textnormal{Rem}_{1}(t)\|_{\ell_{m}^{1*}}\ \leqslant\ C\,t\,e^{C\lambda R\left\langle t\right\rangle}\,\bigg(\lambda R^{2}(R^{\frac{1}{2}}+n^{2})\left\langle t\right\rangle+\frac{R^{3}}{p_{F}^{m}}\bigg)\ .

Remark 2.6
.

At time zero, there holds
Q_{0}=B_{0}=0
.
Hence, in order to prove that the collision operators dominate the remainders terms, we consider longer time scales in the next subsection,
for three dimensional boxes.
We show that for appropiately chosen initial data,
and for
t\gg 1:

(2.39)

c_{\Lambda}\,t\,N^{1/3}\ \leqslant\ \|B_{t}[f_{0}]\|_{\ell_{m}^{1*}}\ \leqslant\ C_{\Lambda}\,t\,N^{1/3}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\|Q_{t}[f_{0}]\|_{\ell_{m}^{1*}}\leqslant tn\ .

We will compare these sizes with the right hand side of
(
2.38
), for
n\ll N^{1/6}
.

Remark 2.7
.

In view of Remark
2.4
, the above result is describing the dynamics
of particles and holes away from the Fermi surface, i.e. on
\Lambda^{*}/\mathcal{S}
.
This is consistent with the fact
[
10
]
that bosonization occurs inside of the Fermi surface.

2.4.
A particular scaling

Let us discuss in this section
a scaling regime for which Theorem
1
turns
into an effective approximation.

Namely, we specialize to the three-dimensional case
amd consider the following scaling regime

(2.40)

\lambda=1/N^{3/2+\delta_{1}},\qquad t=N^{1/6+\delta_{2}}T\ ,\qquad L\ \textnormal{ fixed }\qquad n\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{6}}

for positive parameters

0<\delta_{1}
,
\delta_{2}<1
specified below.

The time scales that we consider are short enough to maintain control over the remainders terms
of Theorem
1
,
but long enough to observe one complete collision.
More precisely, the mollified delta function
\delta_{t}(\Delta E)

incorporated in the definition of
Q_{t}
and
B_{t}
,
now becomes a Kronecker delta function with respect to the free energy.
In other words, we prove in Lemma
10.1
in a suitable sense that

(2.41)

\delta_{t}(\Delta E)=\frac{2t}{\pi}\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Delta e)\Big(1+\mathcal{O}(1/t^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{2}t^{2})\Big)

where

(2.42)

\Delta e\equiv e(p_{1})+e(p_{2})-e(p_{3})-e(p_{4})\ ,\qquad\textnormal{where}\qquad e(p)\equiv\big[\chi^{\perp}(p)-\chi(p)\big]\frac{p^{2}}{2}\ .

As a consequence,
in Lemma
10.2
and
10.3

we are able to identify the leading order terms of the operators
Q_{t}
and
B_{t}

as follows

(2.43)

\displaystyle B_{t}[f]

\displaystyle=t\mathscr{B}[f]+\mathcal{O}_{\ell^{\infty}}(1/t)+\mathcal{O}_{\ell^{\infty}}(t^{3}\lambda^{2}\|\hat{V}\|_{\ell^{1}}^{2})

\displaystyle Q_{t}[f]

\displaystyle=t\mathscr{Q}[f]+\mathcal{O}_{\ell^{\infty}}(1/t)+\mathcal{O}_{\ell^{\infty}}(t^{3}\lambda^{2}\|\hat{V}\|_{\ell^{1}}^{2})\ .

where
f\in\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*}).

Here, the operator
\mathscr{B}[f]

is defined as in Definition

2

but with
\delta_{t}(\Delta E)

replaced by

(2/\pi)\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Delta e)
.
The definition of
\mathscr{Q}
is anologous.

In this context, the following result now follows as a corollary of Theorem
1
,
Lemma
10.2
and
10.3
,
and the inequalities found in Eq. (
2.35
).

Theorem 2

(First collision time)
.

Let
f_{t}(p)
be the momentum distribution of particles and holes,
as given in Definition
1
.
We assume that
Condition
1

and

2
are satisfied,
and consider the scaling (
2.40
) in three dimensions.
Assume that
\delta_{2}\leqslant\delta_{1}/2\equiv\delta
.
Then, for all
m>5

there exists
C>0

such that for all
T\in[N^{-\delta/2},1]

there holds

(2.44)

f_{t}=f_{0}+\lambda^{2}t^{2}\Big(\mathscr{B}[f_{0}]+\mathscr{Q}[f_{0}]+\textnormal{Rem}_{2}(T)\Big)

where
\textnormal{Rem}_{2}
is a remainder term that satisfies

(2.45)

\|\textnormal{Rem}_{2}(T)\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda^{*}\backslash\mathcal{S})}\leqslant CN^{\frac{1}{3}}\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{\delta}}+\frac{1}{N^{(m-5)/3}}\bigg)=o(N^{\frac{1}{3}})\ .

Remark 2.8
.

Theorem
2

describes
f_{t}(p)

for
p\in\Lambda^{*}\backslash\mathcal{S}
, i.e.
away from the Fermi surface.
For
p\in\mathcal{S}
and
T\in[0,1]
,
one actually has an
\ell^{1}
-bound

(2.46)

\|f_{\epsilon^{-1}T}\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathcal{S})}\,\leqslant\,C/N^{5/3+2\delta}

which follows as a propagation-in-time of the depletion of the Fermi surface, as stated in Condition
2
for the initial data

f_{0}
.
See Proposition
5.2
.
In words, the scaling is chosen so that the Fermi surface remains almost entirely depleted over the scale
T.

Remark 2.9

(Sizes of
\mathscr{Q}
and
\mathscr{B}
)
.

The inequality contained in Theorem
2

shows that
\mathscr{B}
and
\mathscr{Q}
dominate the remainder terms if

(2.47)

\|\mathscr{Q}[f_{0}]+\mathscr{B}[f_{0}]\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda^{*}\backslash\mathcal{S})}\,\gg\,\|\textnormal{Rem}_{2}(T)\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda^{*}\backslash\mathcal{S})}\ .

In Section
10

we prove that this holds
for initial data satisfying additionally Condition
4
,
and
\hat{V}(k)
satisfies additionally
Condition
3
.
Let us further explain.

(1)

We take
f(p)
as a linear combination of Kronecker deltas in
\Lambda^{*}
(see Def.
9
). Further, we assume that they are supported
away from each other by a distance
r>0
,
and that at least one of their cartesian components satisfies
|p_{i}|\sim p_{F}
.
We show that

(2.48)

c_{\Lambda}N^{1/3}\ \leqslant\ \|\mathscr{B}[f]\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda^{*}/\mathcal{S})}\ \leqslant\ C_{\Lambda}N^{1/3}

where
c_{\Lambda},C_{\Lambda}>0
.
The heuristics for the
N^{1/3}
dependence are as follows: given
k\in\textnormal{supp}\hat{V}/\{0\}

a fermion can interact with any of the particle-hole pairs in the lune set

(2.49)

L(k)\equiv\{q\in\Lambda^{*}:|q|\leqslant p_{F},\ |q+k|\geqslant p_{F}\}\ ,

which is of order
|L(k)|\sim N^{2/3}
.
On the other hand, energy conservation
\delta_{\mathbb{Z}}(\Delta e)

introduces a geometric constraint that reduces this number by a factor
N^{1/3}
.

(2)

A rather standard bound for
f\in\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*})
with
0\leqslant f\leqslant 1
shows that

(2.50)

\|\mathscr{Q}[f]\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda^{*})}\leqslant C\|f\|_{\ell^{1}(\Lambda_{*})}=Cn\ ,

which is sufficient
to conclude the validity of (
2.47
)

2.5.
The original distribution function

Let us now state our final result, regarding the expansion
(
1.10
) introduced in the first section.

Theorem 3
.

Let
F_{t}(p)
be the momentum distribution of the system,
defined in
(
2.5
).
Assume that
Condition
1

and

2
are satisfied,
and consider the scaling (
2.40
)
in three dimensions,
with

\delta_{2}\leqslant\delta_{1}/2=\delta
.
Then, for all
m>5
there exists

C>0

such that

(2.51)

F_{t}=F_{0}+\lambda^{2}t^{2}\Big(\mathscr{C}[F_{0}]+\textnormal{Rem}_{3}(T)\Big)\qquad\forall T\in[N^{-\delta/2},1]

where
\textnormal{Rem}_{3}(T)

is a remainder term
that satisfies

(2.52)

\|\textnormal{Rem}_{3}(T)\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda^{*})}\leqslant CN^{\frac{1}{3}}\bigg(\frac{1}{N^{\delta}}+\frac{1}{N^{(m-5)/3}}+\frac{1}{N^{1/6}}\bigg)=o(N^{\frac{1}{3}})\ .

Additionally, if
F_{0}
and
\hat{V}
satisfy Condition
3
and
4
, respectively,
there exist positive constants
c_{\Lambda},C_{\Lambda}>0

such that

(2.53)

c_{\Lambda}N^{1/3}\ \leqslant\ \|\mathscr{C}[F_{0}]\|_{\ell^{\infty}(\Lambda^{*}\backslash\mathcal{S})}\ \leqslant\ C_{\Lambda}N^{1/3}\ .

Remark 2.10
.

Remark
2.8

implies that
\|F_{t}-\chi\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathcal{S})}\leqslant CN^{-5/3-2\delta}
.
That is, the Fermi surface remains almost stationary.

2.6.
Comparison

Let us compare our work
with previous results that are available in the literature.

(1)

Benedikter, Nam, Porta, Schlein and Seiringer
[
10
]

studied the dynamics of a three-dimensional Fermi gas around the Fermi ball,
in the semi-classical
\hbar=N^{-1/3}
, mean-field regime

\lambda=1/N
.
The main focus is on the
bosonization
of particle-hole pairs inside of a suitable neighborhood of the Fermi surface.
The initial states that are considered arise naturally as thermal fluctuations of the ground state problem.
In fact, their methods
have been used to prove upper and lower bounds
for the correlation energy in the so-called Random Phase Approximation.
In their terms, here instead we consider the contribution to the dynamics associated to the “non-bosonizable terms”. These interaction terms describe the dynamics of particles and holes away from the Fermi surface.
See also

[
8
,
9
,
10
,
13
,
35
,
25
,
26
,
27
]

for related results in this direction.

(2)

Hott and the second author
[
18
]

have studied the emergence of quantum Boltzmann dynamics for the fluctuations around a Bose-Einstein condensate.
In particular, our scaling regimes are similar to one another, in the sense that both of them contain a large number of particles per unit volume.
In other words, the density of particles acts as an expansion parameter.
Similarly, they both employ quasi-free initial states.
While of course the difference in statistics plays a crucial role in the analysis,
the approach presented here is largely inspired by that of
[
18
]
.
See also
[
19
]

for a more recent refined analysis
which incorporates additional renormalized terms
into the Hartree-Fock-Bogoliuboc dynamics,
yielding control over longer time-scales.

(3)

Erdős
[
30
]
studied the weak-coupling limit
of an electron interacting with a thermal bath of phonons. In particular,
a linear Boltzmann equation is shown to emerge from the long-time dynamics.
It turns out that this is not very different than the situation under consideration.
Namely, the dynamics of particles and holes
outside of the Fermi surface can also be described as particles
that interact with a boson field, i.e. the bosonized electron-hole pairs around the Fermi surface, as described by
[
10
]
.
The situation here is more complicated, however. On the one hand, bosonization is only approximate. On the other hand, several other interactions influence the dynamics of particles and holes,
and rigorous error control over these interactions is already demanding.
Finally, let us recall that the weak coupling limit of electrons interacting with a random medium is intimately related to the model studied in
[
30
]
; for results in this direction see e.g

[
17
,
20
,
21
,
31
,
47
]
.

(4)

The scaling regime
(
2.40
) contains an interaction strength that is
much
weaker than the microscopic mean-field scaling regime–of order
N^{-1/3}
in three dimensions for fermions.
Here instead, the kinetic and potential energies
are not in balance
over equal time scales,
and
it is necessary to consider longer time scales in order
to observe the aggregated effect
of particle interactions.
The literature of mean-field theory for fermions is vast, see e.g

[
1
,
2
,
34
,
42
,
11
,
12
,
29
,
33
,
43
,
37
,
40
,
10
,
8
,
13
,
25
,
35
]

for a non-exhaustive list of references.

3.
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce preliminaries that are needed to prove our main result.
First, we give an explicit representation of the particle-hole Hamiltonian
\mathfrak{h}
, introduced in (
2.12
).
Second, based on this representation, we introduce the interaction picture framework that we shall use to study the dynamics of the momentum distribution
f_{t}(p)
, defined in (
2.13
).
Third, we perform a double commutator expansion and identify nine terms,
from which we shall extract leading order and subleading order terms.
Finally, we introduce number estimates that we use to analyze the nine terms found in the double commutator expansion.

3.1.
Calculation of
\mathfrak{h}

Let us introduce two fundamental collection of operators.
We shall refer to them informally as
D
- and
b
-operators, respectively.

Definition 4
.

Let
k\in\Lambda^{*}
.

(1)
We define the
D
-operators as

(3.1)

D_{k}\equiv\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p)\chi^{\perp}(p-k)a_{p-k}^{*}a_{p}\,\mathrm{d}p-\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi(h)\chi(h+k)a_{h+k}^{*}a_{h}\,\mathrm{d}h\ .

(2)
We define the
b
-operators as

(3.2)

b_{k}\equiv\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p)\chi(p-k)a_{p-k}a_{p}\,\mathrm{d}p\ .

Remark 3.1
.

For the rest of the article, we denote the corresponding adjoint operators by

D_{k}^{*}\equiv[D_{k}]^{*}
and
b^{*}_{k}\equiv[b_{k}]^{*}
, respectively.
Additionally, we shall extensively use the basic relation

(3.3)

D^{*}_{k}=D_{-k}\qquad\forall k\in\Lambda^{*}\ .

Remark 3.2

(Heuristics)
.

One should understand the operator
D
as a combination fermionic operators; they intertwine only holes and holes, together with particles and particles,
that are away from the Fermi surface.
One the other hand, the operators
b
should be understood as an approximate bosonic operators; they create/annihilate bosonized particle-hole pairs near the Fermi surface.
In fact, the following commutation relation holds

(3.4)

[b_{k},D^{*}_{k}]=0\qquad\forall k\in\Lambda^{*}\ .

However, we shall not need any estimates on the
commutation relations satisfied by
b
’s, and this interpretation will remain at a heuristic level.

The following lemma contains the explicit representation for the particle-hole Hamiltonian, in terms of
a “solvable Hamiltonian”, plus interaction terms depending on
D
and
b
operators.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\mathfrak{h}
be the operator defined in (
2.12
).
Then, the following identity holds

(3.5)

\mathfrak{h}-\mu_{1}\mathds{1}-\mu_{2}\mathcal{Q}=\mathfrak{h}_{0}+\lambda\mathcal{V}

for some real-valued constants
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Here

\mathcal{Q}

corresponds to the
charge operator

(3.6)

\mathcal{Q}\equiv\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p)a_{p}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p-\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi(p)a_{p}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p;

\mathfrak{h}_{0}
corresponds to the quadratic, diagonal operator

(3.7)

\mathfrak{h}_{0}=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}E_{p}a_{p}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p

with
E_{p}
the dispersion relation defined in (
2.20
);
and
\mathcal{V}=V_{F}+V_{FB}+V_{B}
contains the following three interaction terms

(3.8)

\displaystyle V_{F}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\hat{V}(k)D^{*}_{k}D_{k}\ \mathrm{d}k

(3.9)

\displaystyle V_{FB}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\hat{V}(k)D^{*}_{k}\big[b_{k}+b^{*}_{-k}\big]\mathrm{d}k

(3.10)

\displaystyle V_{B}

\displaystyle\equiv\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\hat{V}(k)\big[b^{*}_{k}b_{k}+\frac{1}{2}\,b^{*}_{k}b^{*}_{-k}+\frac{1}{2}\,b_{-k}b_{k}\big]\mathrm{d}k\ .

Remark 3.3
.

The labeling of
V_{F}
,
V_{FB}
and
V_{B}
is of course related to Remark
3.2
. Namely,
V_{F}
contains fermion/fermion interactions,
V_{FB}
contains fermion/boson interactions and
V_{B}
contains boson/boson interactions.

Remark 3.4
.

The charge operator
\mathcal{Q}
is irrelevant for the dynamics in the system.
Indeed, one may easily check that
[\mathfrak{h}_{0},\mathcal{Q}]=[D,\mathcal{Q}]=[b,\mathcal{Q}]=0
and, therefore,

[\mathfrak{h},\mathcal{Q}]=0
. In other words, the charge is a constant of motion
and only the right hand side of (
3.5
) is relevant regarding the time evolution of the momenum distribution of the system.
We make this argument precise in the next subsubsection.

The proof of the above Lemma will not be given here, for it has already been considered in the literature
in a very similary form. The reader is refered for instance to
[
9
, pps 897-899]
.

3.2.
The interaction picture

Let us now exploit the identity found in (
3.5
).
First, recalling that the Hamiltonian
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
is quadratic and diagonal with respect to creation and annihilation operators,
we may easily calculate the associated Heisenberg evolution to be given by

(3.11)

\displaystyle a_{p}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv e^{it\mathfrak{h}_{0}}a_{p}e^{-it\mathfrak{h}_{0}}=e^{-itE_{p}}a_{p}\ ,

(3.12)

\displaystyle a_{p}^{*}(t)

\displaystyle\equiv e^{it\mathfrak{h}_{0}}a_{p}^{*}e^{-it\mathfrak{h}_{0}}=e^{+itE_{p}}a_{p}^{*}\ ,

for all
p\in\Lambda^{*}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
; the dispersion relation
E_{p}
was defined in (
2.20
).
Secondly, we introduce the

interaction Hamiltonian

(3.13)

\mathfrak{h}_{I}(t)\equiv\lambda\,e^{it\mathfrak{h}_{0}}\mathcal{V}e^{-it\mathfrak{h}_{0}}\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\ ,

where
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
and
\mathcal{V}
are defined in Lemma
3.5
.

We now introduce the dynamics associated to the interaction picture.

Definition 5
.

Given an initial state
\nu:B(\mathscr{F})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we denote by
(\nu_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{R}}

the solution of the intial value problem

(3.14)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&i\partial_{t}\nu_{t}(\mathcal{O})=\nu_{t}\big([\mathfrak{h}_{I}(t),\mathcal{O}]\big)\quad\forall\mathcal{O}\in B(\mathscr{F})\\
&\nu_{0}=\nu\end{cases}

which we shall refer to as the interaction dynamics.

The momentum distribution of the system
f_{t}(p)
,
introduced in Def.
1
,
is now linked to the interaction dynamics.
Indeed, a standard calculation shows that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and

p\in\Lambda^{*}
, there holds

(3.15)

f_{t}(p)=|\Lambda|^{-1}{\nu_{t}(a_{p}^{*}a_{p})}\ .

In the next subsection, we shall use Eq. (
3.15
)
to expand
f_{t}(p).

3.3.
Double commutator expansion

Let
f_{t}(p)
be as in Eq. (
3.15
),
and let us recall that
\nu
is an initial state satisfying Condition
2
.
In particular, quasi-freeness and translation invariance imply that

(3.16)

\nu([a_{p}^{*}a_{p},a_{k_{1}}^{\hash}a_{k_{2}}^{\hash}a_{k_{3}}^{\hash}a_{k_{4}}^{\hash}])=0\ ,\qquad\forall\,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}\in\Lambda^{*}\ .

Thus, upon expressing the Hamiltonian
\mathfrak{h}_{I}(t)

in terms of creation- and annihilation operators,
one finds that

\partial_{t}|_{t=0}f_{t}(p)=i|\Lambda|^{-1}\nu([a_{p}^{*}a_{p},\mathfrak{h}_{I}(0)])=0
.
Hence,
the following third-order expansion holds true

(3.17)

f_{t}(p)=f_{0}(p)-|\Lambda|^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\nu_{t_{2}}\Big([[a_{p}^{*}a_{p},\mathfrak{h}_{I}(t_{1})],\mathfrak{h}_{I}(t_{2})]\Big)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}

for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in\Lambda^{*}.

We dedicate the rest of this article to the study of the right-hand side of the above equation.

Let us identify all of the terms in the double commutator expansion found above.
A straightforward expansion of the interaction Hamiltonian yields the decomposition

(3.18)

\mathfrak{h}_{I}(t)=\lambda\big(V_{F}(t)+V_{FB}(t)+V_{B}(t)\big)\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}

where the interaction terms above according to the Heisenberg picture. Namely, we set

(3.19)

V_{\alpha}(t)\equiv\,e^{it\mathfrak{h}_{0}}V_{\alpha}\,e^{-it\mathfrak{h}_{0}}\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\ ,\alpha\in\{F,FB,B\}\ .

Upon expanding the right hand side of
(
3.17
),
one finds the following nine terms

\displaystyle f_{t}-f_{0}=

\displaystyle-\lambda^{2}|\Lambda|^{-1}\Big(T_{F,F}(t)+T_{F,FB}(t)+T_{F,B}(t)\Big)

\displaystyle-\lambda^{2}|\Lambda|^{-1}\Big(T_{FB,F}(t)+T_{FB,FB}(t)+T_{FB,B}(t)\Big)

(3.20)

\displaystyle-\lambda^{2}|\Lambda|^{-1}\Big(T_{B,F}(t)+T_{B,FB}(t)+T_{B,B}(t)\Big)

where we set, for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in\Lambda^{*}

(3.21)

\displaystyle T_{\alpha,\beta}(t,p)\equiv\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\nu_{t_{2}}\Big([[a_{p}^{*}a_{p},V_{\alpha}(t_{1})],V_{\beta}(t_{2})]\Big)\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\qquad\alpha,\beta\in\{F,FB,B\}\ .

We shall analyze in detail the quantities
T_{\alpha,\beta}:\mathbb{R}\times\Lambda^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

when tested against a smooth function.
To this end, let us introduce some notation we shall be using for the rest of this work.
For
\varphi:\Lambda^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
we let

(3.22)

N(\varphi)\equiv\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\overline{\varphi(p)}a_{p}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p

together with

(3.23)

\displaystyle T_{\alpha,\beta}(t,\varphi)\equiv\left\langle\varphi,T_{\alpha,\beta}(t)\right\rangle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\nu_{t_{2}}\Big([[N(\varphi),V_{\alpha}(t_{1})],V_{\beta}(t_{2})]\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\ .

3.4.
Excitation operators

The following two operators will play a major role in our analysis.
They correspond to the number operator (per unit volume) that counts the total number of particles and holes in the system,
together with the number operator that only counts the number of particles and hole in the Fermi surface
\mathcal{S}
.
More precisely, we consider

Definition 6
.

We define the two following operators in
\mathscr{F}
.

(1) The number operator as

(3.24)

\mathcal{N}\equiv\int_{\Lambda^{*}}a_{p}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p\ .

(2) The
surface-localized number operator as

(3.25)

\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}}\equiv\int_{\mathcal{S}}a_{p}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p

where
\mathcal{S}
is the Fermi surface, defined in (
2.16
) .

Remark 3.5
.

Let us recall that in Section
2

we have introduced the parameter
R=|\Lambda|\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mathrm{d}p
.
In particular, it follows from the boundedness of creation- and annihilation-
operators
that
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}}
is a bouned operator
and

\|\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}}\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}\leqslant R
.

Remark 3.6

(Domains)
.

\mathcal{N}
is an unbounded
self-adjoint operator in
\mathscr{F}

with domain

\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{N})=\{\Psi=(\psi_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}\in\mathscr{F}:\sum_{n\geqslant 0}n^{2}\|\psi_{n}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\Lambda^{n})}<\infty\}\ .

As initial data, the mixed states that we work with
satisfy

(3.26)

\nu(\mathcal{N})\equiv\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\nu(a_{p}^{*}a_{p})\mathrm{d}p=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}f_{0}(p)\mathrm{d}p<\infty\ ,

and similarly for higher powers
\mathcal{N}^{k}
.
It is standard to show that
the time evolution generated by the particle-hole Hamiltonian
\mathfrak{h}
,
as defined in (
2.12
),
preserves
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{N})
,
in the sense that
\nu_{t}(\mathcal{N}^{k})<\infty

for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
In order to simplify the exposition,
we shall purposefuly not refer to the unbounded nature
of the operator
\mathcal{N}
in the rest of the article.

The proof of Theorem
1
relies on the fact that
the subleading order terms that arise from the double commutator expansion –written in terms of
b
- and
D
-operators–
can be bounded above by expectations of the operators
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}}
,
with respect to the evolution of the state
\nu
driven by the interaction Hamiltonian
\mathfrak{h}_{I}(t)
.
This analysis is carried out in Section
4
.
Further, in Section
5

we prove bounds for the growth-in-time of
the expectations
\nu_{t}(\mathcal{N})
and
\nu_{t}(\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}})
.
This two-step analysis is combined in Section
9
to prove Theorem
1
.

4.
Tool Box I: Analysis of
b
- and
D
-operators

In the last section, we introduced
the time evolution of certain observables in the Heisenberg picture,
with respect to the solvable Hamiltonian
\mathfrak{h}_{0}
, introduced in (
3.7
).
In particular, the evolution of the creation- and annihilation- operators
a
and
a^{*}

take the simple form

(4.1)

\displaystyle a_{p}(t)=e^{-itE_{p}}a_{p}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad a_{p}^{*}(t)=e^{+itE_{p}}a_{p}^{*}\ ,

for all
p\in\Lambda^{*}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
; the dispersion relation
E_{p}
was defined in (
2.20
).
Let us now introduce the Heisenberg evolution of the
b
- and
D
-operators
as follow.

Definition 7
.

Let
k\in\Lambda^{*}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

(1)
The Heisenberg evolution of the
D
-operators is given by

D_{k}(t)\equiv e^{it\mathfrak{h}_{0}}D_{k}e^{-it\mathfrak{h}_{0}}=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p,p-k)a_{p-k}^{*}(t)a_{p}(t)\,\mathrm{d}p-\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi(h,h+k)a_{h+k}^{*}(t)a_{h}(t)\,\mathrm{d}h

and
D_{k}^{*}(t)\equiv[D_{k}(t)]^{*}=D_{-k}(t)
.

(2)
The Heisenberg evolution of the
b
-operators is given by

b_{k}(t)\equiv e^{it\mathfrak{h}_{0}}b_{k}e^{-it\mathfrak{h}_{0}}=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p)\chi(p-k)a_{p-k}(t)a_{p}(t)\,\mathrm{d}p

and
b_{k}^{*}(t)\equiv[b_{k}(t)]^{*}
.

The main goal of this section is to introduce a systematic calculus that lets us deal with combination of the operators
b_{k}(t)
and
D_{k}(t)
–together with multiple combination of their commutators–
as they show up in the analysis of the double commutator expansion found in
(
3.20
).
First, we introduce many useful identities required for the upcoming analysis.
Secondly, we state estimates for several combinations of
b
- and
D
-operators.

4.1.
Identities

In this subsection, we record useful identities between operators in
\mathscr{F}
that we shall use extensively in the rest of this article.
Most importantly, in the next subsection we shall use these identities to obtain estimates of importantes commutator observables.

Preliminary identities
.
First, we write general time-independent relations.

1) For all
p,q,r\in\Lambda^{*}
the CAR imply that

(4.2)

\displaystyle[a_{r}^{*}a_{r},a_{p}^{*}a_{q}]=\big(\delta(r-q)-\delta(r-p)\big)a_{p}^{*}a_{q}

2) For all
p,q\in\Lambda^{*}
and
\varphi\in\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*})
there holds

(4.3)

[N(\varphi),a_{p}^{*}a_{q}]=\Big(\overline{\varphi(p)}-\overline{\varphi(q)}\Big)a_{p}^{*}a_{q}

where we recall
N(\varphi)=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\overline{\varphi(p)}a_{p}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p.

Commutator identities
.
The following lemma contains useful operator identities, to be used in the next section.
Since they only rely on the CAR and straightforward commutator calculations,
we leave their proof to the reader.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
k,\ell\in\Lambda^{*}
and
t,s\in\mathbb{R}
.

(1) For
p\in\mathcal{B}^{c}
and
h\in\mathcal{B}
there holds

(4.4)

\displaystyle[b_{k}(s),a_{p}^{*}(t)]

\displaystyle\ =\ \chi(p-k)\,e^{i(t-s)E_{p}}a_{p-k}(s)\ ,

(4.5)

\displaystyle[b_{k}(s),a_{h}^{*}(t)]

\displaystyle\ =\ -\chi^{\perp}(h+k)\,e^{i(t-s)E_{h}}a_{h+k}(s)\ .

(2) There holds

\displaystyle[b_{\ell}(s),D_{k}^{*}(t)]

\displaystyle=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p)\chi^{\perp}(p-k)\chi(p-\ell)e^{i(t-s)E_{p}}a_{p-\ell}(s)a_{p-k}(t)\mathrm{d}p

(4.6)

\displaystyle+\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi(h)\chi(h+k)\chi^{\perp}(h+\ell)e^{i(t-s)E_{h}}a_{h+\ell}(s)a_{h+k}(t)\mathrm{d}h\ .

In particular,
[b_{k}(t),D_{k}^{*}(s)]=0
.

(3) There holds

\displaystyle[b_{k}(t),b_{\ell}^{*}(s)]

\displaystyle=\ \delta(k-\ell)\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p)\chi(p-k)e^{-i(t-s)(E_{p}+E_{p-k})}\mathrm{d}p

\displaystyle-\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p)\chi^{\perp}(p+\ell-k)\chi(p-k)e^{-i(t-s)E_{p-k}}a_{p}^{*}(t)a_{p+\ell-k}(s)\,\mathrm{d}p

(4.7)

\displaystyle-\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi(h)\chi(h+\ell-k)\chi^{\perp}(h+\ell)e^{-i(t-s)E_{h+k}}a_{h}^{*}(t)a_{h+\ell-k}(s)\,\mathrm{d}h\ .

Besides the
b
- and
D
- operators, we shall work extensively with
their
contracted
versions. These are defined as follows
in terms of
N(\varphi)=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\overline{\varphi(p)}a_{p}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p.

Definition 8

(Contractions)
.

Let
\varphi:\Lambda^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
.
Then, we define the
D(\varphi)
-operators as the collection of operators for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\Lambda^{*}

(4.8)

\displaystyle D_{k}(t,\varphi)\equiv[N(\varphi),D_{k}(t)]\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad D_{k}^{*}(t,\varphi)\equiv[N(\varphi),D_{k}^{*}(t)]\ .

Similarly, we define the
b(\varphi)
-operators
as the collection of operators for
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\Lambda^{*}

(4.9)

\displaystyle b_{k}(t,\varphi)\equiv[N(\varphi),b_{k}(t)]\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad b_{k}^{*}(t,\varphi)\equiv[N(\varphi),b_{k}^{*}(t)]\ .

We call them the contractions of
b
and
D
with the
external function
\varphi
.

Remark 4.1
.

We immediately note that

(4.10)

\displaystyle[D_{k}(t,\varphi)]^{*}=-D^{*}_{k}(t,\bar{\varphi})\qquad[b_{k}(t,\varphi)]^{*}=-b^{*}_{k}(t,\bar{\varphi})

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in\Lambda^{*}
.
Thus, the contractions are not adjoints of each other.
However, the following relations hold true for all
\Psi\in\mathscr{F}

(4.11)

\|[D_{k}(t,\varphi)]^{*}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}=\|D_{k}^{*}(t,\bar{\varphi})\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\|[b_{k}(t,\varphi)]^{*}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}=\|b_{k}^{*}(t,\bar{\varphi})\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}\ .

Since final estimates are given in terms of
\ell^{p}
norms of
\varphi
, the complex conjugation
does not affect the end result.
Thus, when proving estimates, one may regard them as adjoints of each other.

Remark 4.2
.

The contractions can of course be calculated explicitly using the CAR.
Let us record here the two following calculations

\displaystyle D_{k}^{*}(t,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p)\chi^{\perp}(p-k)\big[\varphi(p)-\varphi(p-k)\big]a_{p}^{*}(t)a_{p-k}(t)\mathrm{d}p

(4.12)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi(h)\chi(h+k)\big[\varphi(h)-\varphi(h+k)\big]a_{h}^{*}(t)a_{h+k}(t)\mathrm{d}h\ ,

(4.13)

\displaystyle b_{k}(t,\varphi)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(q)\chi(q-k)\Big[\varphi(q-k)+\varphi(q)\Big]a_{q-k}(t)a_{q}(t)\ \mathrm{d}q\ .

Let us now state in the following Lemmas
some useful commutation relations.
Since they all follow from straightforward manipulation of the CAR,
we leave them as an exercise for the reader.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
k,\ell\in\Lambda^{*}
,
t,s\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varphi\in\ell^{1}
.

(1) There holds

(4.14)

\displaystyle[b_{\ell}(s),

\displaystyle D_{k}^{*}(t,\varphi)]

\displaystyle=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p)\chi^{\perp}(p-k)\chi(p-\ell)\big[\varphi(p)-\varphi(p-k)\big]e^{i(t-s)E_{p}}a_{p-\ell}(s)a_{p-k}(t)\mathrm{d}p

\displaystyle+\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi(h)\chi(h+k)\chi^{\perp}(h+\ell)\big[\varphi(h)-\varphi(h+k)\big]e^{i(t-s)E_{h}}a_{h+\ell}(s)a_{h+k}(t)\mathrm{d}h\ .

Lemma 4.3

(
\mathcal{N}
commutators)
.

For all
k\in\Lambda^{*}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}

the following holds true.

(1) For the
D
-operators

(4.15)

[D_{k}(t),\mathcal{N}]=[D_{k}^{*}(t),\mathcal{N}]=0

and similarly for the contracted operators

D_{k}(t,\varphi)
and
D_{k}^{*}(t,\varphi)
.

(2) For the
b
-operators,
for any measurable function
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
the
pull-through formulae holds true

(4.16)

\displaystyle f(\mathcal{N})b_{k}(t)=b_{k}(t)f(\mathcal{N}-2)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad f(\mathcal{N})b_{k}^{*}(t)=b_{k}^{*}(t)f(\mathcal{N}+2)

and similarly for the contracted operators

b_{k}(t,\varphi)

and

b_{k}^{*}(t,\varphi)
.

Lemma 4.4

(
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}}
commutators)
.

For all
k\in 3\textnormal{supp}\hat{V}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}

the following commutation relations hold true

(4.17)

\displaystyle[\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}},b_{k}(t)]=-2b_{k}(t)\quad\textnormal{and}\quad[\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}},b_{k}^{*}(t)]=+2b_{k}^{*}(t)\ .

4.2.
Estimates

In this subsection we state estimates that shall be used extensively for the rest of this article.
Most of these are operator estimates for observables in
\mathscr{F}
containing the fermionic creation-
and annihilation- operators
a_{p}
and
a_{p}^{*}
.
We remind the reader that these are bounded operators with norm

\|a_{p}\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}\,=\,\|a_{p}^{*}\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}\ \leqslant\ |\Lambda|^{1/2}

for all
p\in\Lambda^{*}\ .

Preliminary estimates
.
Let us state without proof elementary estimates that we shall make use of.

1) For any function
f:\Lambda^{*}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
,

k\in\Lambda^{*}
and
\Psi\in\mathscr{F}
there holds

(4.18)

\Big\|\int_{\Lambda^{*}}f(p)a_{p+k}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p\Psi\,\Big\|_{\mathscr{F}}\leqslant\ \|f\|_{\ell^{\infty}}\|\mathcal{N}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}\ .

2) The Heisenberg evolution of the creation- and annihilation- operators
a_{p}(t)
and
a_{p}^{*}(t)
are bounded operators in
\mathscr{F}
, with norms

(4.19)

\|a_{p}(t)\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}\,=\,\|a_{p}^{*}(t)\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}\ \leqslant\ |\Lambda|^{1/2},\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R},\ p\in\Lambda^{*}\ .

3)
The Heisenberg evolution of the

b
-operators are bounded operators in
\mathscr{F}
with norms

(4.20)

\|b_{k}(t)\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}\,=\,\|b^{*}_{k}(t)\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}\ \leqslant\ |\Lambda|\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p)\chi(p-k)\mathrm{d}p\ \lesssim\ R

for all
k\in\textnormal{supp}\hat{V}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Let us recall that
R=|\Lambda|p_{F}^{d-1}
.

Commutator estimates
.
Let us now describe the most important estimates concerning
b
- and
D
-operators.
Essentially, commutators between
b
- and
D
-operators–together with their contracted versions
b(\varphi)
and
D(\varphi)
–can be classified into four types, depending on the estimate they verify.
It turns out that these four type of estimate exhaust
all
possibilities that show up
in the double commutator expansion for
f_{t}(p)
.
In other words, these estimates are enough to analyze the nine terms

\{T_{\alpha,\beta}(t,p)\}_{\alpha,\beta\in\{F,FB,B\}}
.

We remind the reader of the relation
D_{k}^{*}(t)=D_{-k}(t)
, valid for all
k\in\Lambda^{*}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
In particular,
all
of the upcoming inequalities are valid if we replace
D
by
D^{*}
.
On the other hand,
we warn the reader that
this property
does not
hold for
b
-operators in general.

The first type of estimate concerns the combination of operators
that are relatively bounded with respect to the number operator
\mathcal{N}=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}a_{p}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p
,
or any of its powers.
We call these
Type-I
estimates.
They are contained in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.5

(Type-I estimates)
.

There exists a constant
C>0

such that for any

\Psi\in\mathscr{F}
,
k,\ell\in\Lambda^{*}
,
and
t,s,r\in\mathbb{R}

the following inequalities hold true

(4.21)

\displaystyle\|D_{k}(t)\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ C\|\mathcal{N}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

(4.22)

\displaystyle\|[D_{k}(t),D_{\ell}(s)]\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ C\|\mathcal{N}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

(4.23)

\displaystyle\|[D_{k}(t),D_{\ell}(s)D_{\ell}(r)]\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ C\|\mathcal{N}^{2}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}\ .

The second type of estimates concerns combination of operators that can be bounded above
by
the surface-localized number operator

\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}}=\int_{\mathcal{S}}a_{p}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p
,
up to pre-factors that can grow with the recurring parameter

R=|\Lambda|p_{F}^{d-1}
.
We call these
Type-II
estimates, and they are contained in the following lemma

Lemma 4.6

(Type-II estimates)
.

There exists a constant
C>0

such that for any
\Psi\in\mathscr{F}
,
k,\ell,q\in\textnormal{supp}\hat{V}
,
and
t,s,r\in\mathbb{R}

the following inequalities hold true

(4.24)

\displaystyle\|b_{k}(t)\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ CR^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\|\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1/2}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

(4.25)

\displaystyle\|[b_{\ell}(t),D_{k}(s)]\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ CR^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\|\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1/2}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

(4.26)

\displaystyle\|[[b_{\ell}(t),D_{k}(s)],D_{q}(r)]\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ CR^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\|\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}}^{1/2}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}\ .

Remark 4.3
.

In certain proofs, it will be convenient to use the upper bound

\mathcal{N}_{S}\leqslant\mathcal{N}.

The reader should then have in mind that the (weaker) version
of the estimates contained in Lemma
4.6
,
in which
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}}
is replaced by
\mathcal{N}
,
also holds true.

The third type of estimate corresponds to combination of
operators that have been contracted with a test function
\varphi\in\ell_{m}^{1}
,
and their operator norm can be bounded above in terms of the integral

(4.27)

\int_{\mathcal{S}}|\varphi(p)|\mathrm{d}p\ \lesssim\ p_{F}^{-m}\|\varphi\|_{\ell_{m}^{1}}\ .

We call these
Type-III
estimates, and they are contained in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.7

(Type-III estimates)
.

Let
m>0
.
There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
k,\ell,q\in\textnormal{supp}\hat{V},\,

t,s,r\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varphi\in\ell^{1}_{m}(\Lambda^{*})

the following inequalities true

(4.28)

\displaystyle\|b_{k}(t,\varphi)\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ C\,|\Lambda|\,p_{F}^{-m}\,\|\varphi\|_{\ell_{m}^{1}}

(4.29)

\displaystyle\|[b_{\ell}(t),D_{k}(s,\varphi)]\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ C\,|\Lambda|\,p_{F}^{-m}\,\|\varphi\|_{\ell_{m}^{1}}

(4.30)

\displaystyle\|[[b_{k}(t),D_{\ell}(s)],D_{q}(r,\varphi)]\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ C\,|\Lambda|\,p_{F}^{-m}\,\|\varphi\|_{\ell_{m}^{1}}

Remark 4.4
.

Type-III estimates are symmetric with respect to the exchange of
b
and
b^{*}
.
This property follows from the relation
\|\mathcal{O}\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}=\|\mathcal{O}^{*}\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}

and the symmetry
D_{k}^{*}(t)=D_{-k}(t)
.

The fourth and final type of estimate corresponds to combination of
operators that have been contracted with a test function
\varphi\in\ell_{m}^{1}
,
and their operator norm can be bounded above in terms of the integral

(4.31)

\int_{\Lambda^{*}}|\varphi(p)|\mathrm{d}p\ =\ \|\varphi\|_{\ell^{1}}\ \lesssim\ \|\varphi\|_{\ell_{m}^{1}}\ ,

and a pre-factor, depending on the volume of the box
|\Lambda|.

We call these
Type-IV
estimates, and they are contained in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.8

(Type-IV estimates)
.

There exists a constant
C>0
such that for all
k,\ell,q\in\Lambda^{*},\,

t,s,r\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varphi\in\ell^{1}(\Lambda^{*})

the following inequalities true the following holds true

(4.32)

\displaystyle\|D_{k}(t,\varphi)\|_{B(\mathscr{F})}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ C\,|\Lambda|\|\varphi\|_{\ell^{1}}

(4.33)

\displaystyle\|[D_{k}(t,\varphi),D_{\ell}(s)]\|_{{B}(\mathscr{F})}

\displaystyle\ \leqslant\ C\,|\Lambda|\|\varphi\|_{\ell^{1}}\ .

4.2.1.
Proof of Lemmata

In this subsection, we provide sketches for the proofs of Lemmas
4.5
,
4.6
,
4.7
and
4.8
.

Sketch of Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

Let us fix
\Psi\in\mathscr{F}
,
k,\ell\in\Lambda^{*}
,
and
t,s,r\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof of (1)
.
We shall make use of the elementary estimate
found in (
4.18
).
To this end, starting from (
7
)
we decompose

(4.34)

D_{k}(t)=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}f^{(1)}(t,k,p)a_{p-k}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p+\int_{\Lambda^{*}}f^{(2)}(t,k,h)a_{h+k}^{*}a_{h}\mathrm{d}h

where

f^{(1)}(t,k,p)=\chi^{\perp}(p,p-k)e^{it(E_{p-k}-E_{p})}

and

f^{(2)}(t,k,h)=\chi(h,h+k)e^{it(E_{h+k}-E_{h})}
.
Clearly,
\|f^{(1)}(t,k)\|_{\ell^{\infty}}=\|f^{(2)}(t,k)\|_{\ell^{\infty}}=1
.
Hence, it follows that

\|D_{k}(t)\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}\leqslant 2\|\mathcal{N}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}
.

Proof of (2)

The proof is extremely similar–it suffices to note that the commutator can be calculated explicitly to be

\displaystyle[D_{k}(t),D_{\ell}(s)]=

\displaystyle\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p,p-\ell,p-k-\ell)e^{i(s-t)E_{p-\ell}}a^{*}_{p-k-\ell}(t)a_{p}(s)\mathrm{d}p

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi^{\perp}(p,p-k,p-k-\ell)e^{i(t-s)E_{p-k}}a^{*}_{p-k-\ell}(s)a_{p}(t)\mathrm{d}p

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi(h,h+\ell,h+k+\ell)e^{i(s-t)E_{h+\ell}}a^{*}_{h+k+\ell}(t)a_{h}(s)\mathrm{d}h

(4.35)

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\chi(h,h+k,h+k+\ell)e^{i(t-s)E_{h+k}}a^{*}_{h+k\ell}(s)a_{h}(t)\mathrm{d}h\ .

Hence, the same argument shows that

\|[D_{k}(t),D_{\ell}(s)]\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}\leqslant 4\|\mathcal{N}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}
.

Proof of (3)
.
For simplicity, let us supress the time labels, and the momentum variables.
In what follows
C>0
is a contant whose value may change from line to line.
We calculate using the previous results, and the commutation relations
[\mathcal{N},D]=0

\displaystyle\|[D,DD]\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|D[D,D]\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}+\|[D,D]D\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C\|\mathcal{N}[D,D]\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}+C\|\mathcal{N}D\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

\displaystyle=C\|[D,D]\mathcal{N}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}+\|CD\mathcal{N}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

(4.36)

\displaystyle\leqslant C\|\mathcal{N}^{2}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}\ .

This finishes the proof.
∎

Sketch of Proof of Lemma
4.6
.

Let us fix
\Psi\in\mathscr{F}
,
k,\ell,q\in\textnormal{supp}\hat{V}
,
and
t,s,r\in\mathbb{R}
.

Let us give the main ideas behind the proof.
Let us recall that
\textnormal{supp}\hat{V}
is contained in a ball of radius
r>0
.
For
n\in\mathbb{N}
, define the Fermi surfaces

(4.37)

\mathcal{S}(n)\equiv\{p\in\Lambda^{*}\ :\ p_{F}-nr\leqslant|p|\leqslant p_{F}+nr\},

and the
number operators
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}(n)}\equiv\int_{\mathcal{S}(n)}a_{p}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p
.
In particular, we are denoting
\mathcal{S}=\mathcal{S}(3)
in (
2.6
).
Given
k,\ell\in\textnormal{supp}\hat{V}
,
consider operators of the form

(4.38)

\displaystyle\beta_{k}\equiv\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\,\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}(1)}(p)\,a_{p+k}a_{p}\,\mathrm{d}p\ ,\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\mathcal{D}_{\ell}\equiv\int_{\Lambda^{*}}a_{p+\ell}^{*}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p\ .

One should think generically of
\beta_{k}
as
b_{k}(t)

and
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
as
D_{\ell}(s)
.
We make the following two observations.
First,
\beta_{k}
can be controlled by
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}(1)}
in the following sense

\displaystyle\|\beta_{k}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

\displaystyle\leqslant|\Lambda|^{\frac{1}{2}}\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}(1)}(p)\|a_{p}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}

\displaystyle\leqslant|\Lambda|^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}(1)}(p)\mathrm{d}p\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big(\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}(1)}(p)\|a_{p}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

(4.39)

\displaystyle\lesssim|\Lambda|^{\frac{1}{2}}p_{F}^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\|\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}(1)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}=R^{\frac{1}{2}}\|\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{S}(1)}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Psi\|_{\mathscr{F}}\ ,

where we used a basic geometric estimate
to find that

\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}(1)}(p)\mathrm{d}p\lesssim p_{F}^{d-1}.

Secondly, the commutator between
\beta_{k}
and
\mathcal{D}_{\ell}
can be calculated to be

(4.40)

\displaystyle[\beta_{k},\mathcal{D}_{\ell}]=\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}(1)}(p-\ell)a_{p+k-\ell}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p+\int_{\Lambda^{*}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}(1)}(p)a_{p+k-\ell}a_{p}\mathrm{d}p\ .

Since both
k,\ell\in\textnormal{supp}\hat{V}
,
it holds that
\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}(1)}(p-\ell)\leqslant\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}(2)}(p)
,
and of course

\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}(1)}(p)\leqslant\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{S}(2)}(p)
.
Consequently, the same argument that we used to obtain (
4.39
) can now be repeated on each term of the above equation
to obtain

(4.41)
